*Dear Oleksandr**,*

Congratulations! Your Stage 1 application demonstrated the critical
thinking and agentic potential we are looking for. You are officially *invited
to Stage 2* of the selection pipeline.

You have until *Friday, 16 October 2026, 23:59 Kyiv time* to complete a
mini pet-project in Python solving a real-world defense problem: Time
Series Analysis of air raid alerts in Ukraine.

We do not expect you to be a domain expert in time series forecasting or
defense analytics. We expect you to use AI to bridge that gap. In this
task, AI is not an ornament. It must be your primary engineering tool.

*Data and evaluation.* Work with the most recent data available, and
evaluate your solution on the latest period. Make sure there is no data
leakage: nothing your model is trained on or uses as an input may come from
after the time at which the forecast is made.

*Optional: text and media data.* You are encouraged, but not required, to
go beyond the alert history and use text or media data, for example news,
official statements or public Telegram channels. The same rule applies: use
only content that was published before the time at which the forecast is
made.

*Submission Requirements *

You must submit your work via the form linked below. Your submission must
include three distinct parts:

   1. The artifact: a link to your GitHub repository containing the final,
   working Python code.
   2. The AI log: a full text export of your conversation history with your
   chosen AI tool (Claude, ChatGPT, Gemini, etc.).
   3. The reflection: A short summary (~100 words) answering three
   questions: What went wrong? How did you adjust your approach? Why is your
   final version better than your first attempt? Please submit as plain text,
   as well.

Deadline: *Friday, 16 October 2026, 23:59 Kyiv time (EEST).*

We evaluate the process, not just the result. Your AI interaction log is
our primary signal. We are looking for candidates who actively iterate,
challenge model hallucinations, correct errors, and guide the AI toward a
functional architecture rather than blindly accepting the first output.

Top applicants who successfully pass this stage will be invited to Stage 3,
which consists of a technical and motivational screening with two
interviewers, where we will also discuss your solution.

*[Stage 2 Submission Form link]* <https://forms.gle/y7fdZsg19XdW7Xtc9>

If you have any questions, please do not hesitate to contact us.

Good luck!

*The Admissions Team*

--0000000000000e5980065dddbb37

- **Alerts.in.ua (volunteer site):** It reported about 14,870 air raid alarms in 2022 and used data from local mass media, "Suspilne", channels of heads of regional administrations, mobile applications, and other public sources. - **Air.alarms.in.ua:** Per Kyiv Post, it has been collecting data since the first day of Russia's full-scale invasion.
- **Telegram-based datasets:** A researcher's talk describes building alert history by exporting chat history from official alert channels, which would need cleaning before use.
- **UAVRadar.live:** It says its historical alert data supports pattern analysis, but the result doesn't mention a CSV export.

**Dataset: Ukrainian air raid sirens (Vadimkin/ukrainian-air-raid-sirens-dataset)**

- **Purpose:** Records of air raid sirens in Ukraine during the Russia invasion, broken down by region. The repository's description is "Dataset of air raid sirens in the Ukraine-Russia war."

The page does not list the CSV filenames or columns. It points to `datasets/README.md` for those details.

- **License:** MIT, per the "MIT license" link.
- **Activity:** 88 stars, 5 forks, 3 open issues.
- **Regeneration:** Users can rebuild the CSVs by running `process.py` after setting up a Python environment and a Telegram app ID and hash in `config.py`.

2026-10-15T13:07:29.994Z | USER | 05198c33-0775-4e41-afb0-b79220bbcec4
[TOOL RESULT: toolu_01UaoBuMyUs7BbABWES7fyEY]
**Endpoints**

- **Active alerts:** `/v1/alerts/active.json` returns detailed records for regions with an active air raid or other threat, including `alert_level` (red/yellow) and a `threats` array in v1 of the record format.
- **Compact status (IoT):**
  - `/v1/iot/active_air_raid_alerts_by_oblast.json` and `/v2/...`: a 27-character status string for oblasts, in a fixed order.
  - `/v1/iot/active_air_raid_alerts/{uid}.json` and `/v2/...`: status for one region by UID.
  - `/v1/iot/active_air_raid_alerts.json` and `/v2/...`: status for all regions and communities, where each character's position matches a location UID.
- **History:** `/v1/regions/{uid}/alerts/{period}.json`. The only documented period is `month_ago`, so history depth is about one month.

The v2 endpoints add red (R) and yellow (Y) levels. The v1 endpoints report any active level as A.

**Token**

Yes, a personal API token is required. You can pass it as a `token` query parameter or as a Bearer token in the `Authorization` header. You request it through a form on the site, and the docs say to keep it secret.

**Rate limits**

- Soft limit: about 8–10 requests per minute per IP.
- Hard limit: 12 requests per minute per IP.
- Exceeding the limit returns HTTP 429. Systematic violations can get the IP and token blocked, and unusual daily volume can also lead to a token block.
- History requests have a separate limit of 2 per minute and are not recommended for real-time use.
- For caching, you can use the `If-Modified-Since` header with `Last-Modified` to fetch data only when it has changed.
- Public services should proxy requests through their own server rather than embed the token in client code. Physical IoT devices are the stated exception.

**Data granularity**

- Locations are typed as oblast, raion, city, hromada, or unknown.
- Alert records include start, end (null while active), and last-updated timestamps.
- Regional status can be full (A/R/Y) or partial (P, meaning some districts or communities).
- Location UIDs are listed for oblasts and a few cities. The full list of districts and cities is in a separate linked spreadsheet.

The docs also warn that updates can be delayed and that the API should not be used for critical infrastructure.

2026-10-15T13:08:57.483Z | ASSISTANT | 889b5ff2-fc69-45c6-9a85-e5fad10cc926
Before anything else: **the deadline is tomorrow (16 Oct, 23:59 Kyiv), so we have about 36 hours.** That favors a simple, correct, well-tested pipeline over an ambitious one. I checked the data sources live today, so what follows is current rather than from memory.

---

## 1. What the assignment actually asks for

**Stated rules:**
- A Python project forecasting air-raid alerts, with working code in a GitHub repo.
- Use **the most recent data** and **evaluate on the latest period**.
- **No leakage:** nothing used for training or as an input can come from after the moment the forecast is made.
- Optional: text or media sources (news, Telegram), under the same rule.
- Deliverables: the repo, the full AI log, and a ~100-word reflection (what went wrong, how you adjusted, why the final version is better).

**What they're really grading:** they say the AI log is the *primary signal*. They want to see you iterate, catch hallucinations and steer the work. So:
- Push back on me when something looks wrong. Don't just accept my output. This conversation is part of your submission.
- Keep a short `DECISIONS.md` as we go. It will make the reflection easy to write, and it prepares you for Stage 3, where they will question you on the solution.
- Reproducibility counts as part of "working code": a pinned data snapshot date, `requirements.txt`, and one command that runs everything.

## 2. Data sources (checked today)

| Source | What it gives | Verdict |
|---|---|---|
| **[Vadimkin/ukrainian-air-raid-sirens-dataset](https://github.com/Vadimkin/ukrainian-air-raid-sirens-dataset)** | Official alerts since 15 Mar 2022 (CSV, UTC, MIT license). A bot commits new data daily; the last commit was **today, 15 Oct 2026**. | **Main source.** Full history and current. |
| Same repo, volunteer data (eTryvoga) | Oblast-level data from 25 Feb 2022. Some alerts have no recorded end and get an imputed +30 min (`naive=True`). | Skip. The official data is cleaner. |
| **[alerts.in.ua API](https://devs.alerts.in.ua/)** | Live status plus about one month of history. Needs a token; rate limits are ~10 req/min, and history calls 2/min. | Useful only for a live demo or a cross-check. Not enough history for training. |
| Telegram channels (official alert channel, monitoring channels that report launches) | Possibly strong leading signals, since launch reports come before alerts. | A stretch goal. Needs Telegram API credentials, which you'd have to create yourself. |

**Two findings that change the design:**
1. **Granularity changed in December 2025.** Before then, official alerts covered **whole oblasts**. Since then they are mostly at **raion level** (about 139 raions). The data has a structural break 10 months ago, which affects both feature design and training-window choice.
2. **Two permanent alerts (Luhansk since Apr 2022, Crimea since Dec 2022) are not in the data at all.** We exclude those regions explicitly instead of letting them appear as "never alerted".

I couldn't see the CSV column names from the README. I expect something like `region, started_at, finished_at`, but we'll confirm when we download. Don't let me assume a schema.

## 3. What to predict

Options, roughly from easiest to hardest:

| Target | Pros | Cons |
|---|---|---|
| **A. Next hour, per oblast: will an alert be active?** | Simple and well-defined. | Close to trivial: "is there an alert now" predicts the next hour very well. That makes it a good sanity check but a weak headline result. |
| **B. Same binary target, horizon of a few hours to 24h** (e.g. P(alert during hour t+h), with h = 1…24) | Useful ("what does tonight look like?"). Persistence stops dominating, so the model has to learn real patterns. | Slightly more complex labeling. |
| C. Daily alert-hours per oblast (regression) | Easy to explain. | Coarse; loses time-of-day patterns, which are strong because Shahed attacks happen mostly at night. |
| D. Time until the next alert starts (survival) | Elegant. | Harder to evaluate and explain within 36h. |

**My recommendation:** a panel of oblast-hours. Forecasts are issued at the top of each hour, and the target is a binary "alert active at any point during hour t+h". Report results for **h = 1** (sanity check) and **h = 6–24** (the actual result). Use **oblast level**, defined as "any raion in the oblast is under alert". That keeps the series comparable across the December 2025 change. Raion level is more granular but gives only 10 months of consistent history.

## 4. Ways to solve it

1. **Baselines (required, not optional):**
   - Persistence: "same as now".
   - Seasonal naive: "same hour yesterday".
   - Climatology: the historical rate per oblast × hour-of-day × day-of-week.

   Without these, an AUC of 0.9 means nothing, because autocorrelation alone gives high scores.
2. **ARIMA/SARIMA/Prophet:** I'd argue against these. They assume one continuous series. We have a binary, intermittent panel of ~24 related series with daily and weekly seasonality, and cross-regional effects (an attack moves across oblasts). They're a poor fit, and Prophet is known to be weak on data like this.
3. **Logistic regression on engineered features:** a cheap, interpretable step above the baselines.
4. **Gradient boosting (LightGBM), one global model across all oblasts:** **recommended as the main model.** Features would be:
   - lags of the oblast's own state
   - time since the last alert, and duration of the current alert
   - the national share of oblasts under alert right now
   - the state of neighboring oblasts
   - local hour and weekday (Kyiv time)
   - oblast ID

   It's fast, handles cross-regional features naturally, and is the standard strong choice for tabular time-series data.
5. **Hawkes process (self-exciting point process):** theoretically the best fit, because alerts trigger more alerts nearby. It's interesting to mention, but too risky for the deadline.
6. **LSTM/TFT/foundation models (Chronos, TimesFM):** overkill. They need a lot of data and tuning, and are hard to debug in 36h. They're also poorly suited to a binary panel.

Plan: **baselines → logistic regression → LightGBM**, compared on the same test set.

## 5. Testing the results

- **Split by time only.** Freeze a snapshot (e.g. data up to 15 Oct 2026).
  - **Test:** the last ~4 weeks, used **once**, at the end.
  - **Validation:** rolling-origin (expanding window) folds before the test period, with a **gap equal to the horizon** between train and validation.
- **Metrics:** because we output probabilities:
  - Brier score and log loss
  - **Brier skill score against climatology** (the headline number)
  - PR-AUC, which handles class imbalance better than ROC-AUC
  - a calibration plot
- **Slices:** frontline oblasts (alerts almost all the time) versus rear oblasts, and "currently no alert" hours, where the model has to predict an alert *starting*. Overall averages hide where the model fails.
- **Training window as a hyperparameter:** 2022 may hurt more than it helps, since attack patterns changed a lot. Let validation choose how much history to use.

## 6. Problems and data leakage

**Leakage traps, ordered by how likely we are to fall into them:**
1. **Using the end time of an ongoing alert.** A feature like "alert minutes in the last 24h" built from `finished_at` uses future information if the alert was still running at time t. Every interval must be **clipped at t**. This is the classic trap for this dataset.
2. **Random or shuffled splits.** Never use them; hourly rows next to each other are almost identical.
3. **Statistics computed on the full dataset.** Climatology baselines, per-oblast target encodings and scalers must be fit on the training data only, and refit for each fold.
4. **Off-by-one labels and rolling windows.** Pandas `rolling` includes the current row; `center=True` looks ahead. Each label must start strictly after the latest timestamp the features use. We'll write an **automated test** that checks this rather than trusting my code.
5. **Timezones.** The data is UTC, but daily patterns follow Kyiv local time, which has daylight saving time. A one-hour shift can quietly make features and labels overlap.
6. **Tuning on the test set.** If we look at test results, tweak, and look again, the test set has become a validation set. We look at it once.
7. **Data revisions.** The CSV we download today may include corrections made after the fact. We can't fully fix this, but we should note it as a limitation.
8. **Text data, if we add it:**
   - Ukrainian Air Force summaries are published the *next morning* about the night's attack, so they can't be used for same-night forecasts.
   - Telegram posts can be edited after publication.
   - News sites often show the event date rather than the publication date.
9. **Weather, if we add it:** actual observed weather at t+h is *future data*. Only weather *forecasts* that were available at t are legal.
10. **The LLM itself:** if an LLM reads or labels news, it may "know" the outcome from its training data. My training data goes up to June 2026 and the test period is September–October 2026, so we're safe here. It's still worth stating in the README.

**Non-leakage problems:** the December 2025 granularity break, the missing permanent alerts, strong class imbalance, and non-stationarity (the war changes, so patterns drift).

---

**Suggested scope for 36h:** core pipeline, baselines, LightGBM and the leakage tests by tomorrow afternoon. Telegram text data only if time remains.

Sources: [Vadimkin dataset](https://github.com/Vadimkin/ukrainian-air-raid-sirens-dataset) · [dataset README](https://github.com/Vadimkin/ukrainian-air-raid-sirens-dataset/blob/main/datasets/README.md) · [alerts.in.ua API docs](https://devs.alerts.in.ua/) · [Kyiv Post analysis](https://www.kyivpost.com/amp/post/18257) · [Babel](https://babel.ua/en/news/88907-in-ukraine-the-number-of-air-alarms-per-year-was-calculated)

2026-10-15T16:46:10.477Z | ASSISTANT | 77ec4de3-d241-49d1-b42a-4b12651bf8da
I compared the two files with throwaway analysis scripts in the scratchpad; no project code yet. Your downloads are byte-identical to the current files in the repo.

## What the data shows

| Check | Official | Volunteer |
|---|---|---|
| **Latest record** | **2026-09-07 05:28 UTC.** About 5.5 weeks stale, even though the repo gets daily commits. | **2026-10-15 03:28 UTC** (today) |
| Missing days | 30 Aug – 1 Sep 2026 have no rows | None |
| **Exact duplicate rows** | **113,845 (38%).** Every row from 2022–2025 appears exactly twice. | 0 |
| Granularity | Mix of oblast, raion and **hromada** rows. 2026 is almost all raion or hromada. | Oblast only |
| Estimated end times | None flagged | **5,877 rows (5%)** with `naive=True`. All of them are exactly start + 30 min, so the end is a placeholder. Mostly frontline: Kherson, Sumy, Donetsk, Kharkiv. The share rose to **9.3% in October**. |
| Suspicious long alerts | Hromada alerts lasting up to **604 days** (a Kharkiv hromada, May 2024 → Jan 2026) | Multi-day alerts in the west, e.g. Zakarpattia, Ternopil and Chernivtsi for 5–8 days in April 2026. Almost certainly missed "all clear" messages, not real alerts. |
| Kyiv City vs Kyiv oblast | Separate | Separate ✅ |

**How well do they agree?**
- **Start times:** only **65–70%** of volunteer alert starts have an official start within ±15 min. When they do match, the official record is about **10–20 seconds earlier**. That fits volunteers relaying official alerts. The timing gap doesn't matter at hourly resolution; the unmatched 30% does.
- **"Region under alert" means different things.** If you take official data as "any raion or hromada in the oblast", Dnipropetrovsk is under alert **99.9%** of the time in 2026 and Kharkiv 86%. That's because some frontline hromada is always alerted. The volunteer data puts both at about 20%. They are measuring different events.
- **Unexplained case:** Volyn in 2026 is under alert 15.8% of the time in the volunteer data but 1.8% in the official data. I can't tell which one is wrong without a third source. alerts.in.ua history could settle it, but only for the last month.

## Your ideas

### Target: "will a new alert start in the next 3 hours" (good choice)
It fixes the saturation problem, and there's a bonus: **it depends mostly on start times, which are the reliable part of both datasets.** End times are where the data is messy (placeholder +30 min ends, multi-day ghost alerts).

Things to settle before coding:
1. **What if an alert is already active at forecast time?** Either predict only for regions that are currently calm (a cleaner "onset" question), or allow "the current one ends and a new one starts". My suggestion: train on all hours with "currently active" as a feature, but **report metrics separately for calm hours**. That's where the model actually has to predict something.
2. **Flapping.** Some regions end an alert and restart it within minutes. Should a restart 2 minutes later count as a *new* alert? I'd merge gaps shorter than ~5–10 min. Otherwise the target rewards predicting noise.
3. **You're half right about "always yes at 6h+".** That's true in the east but not in the west, where Volyn and Lviv are under alert about 2% of the time. Even at 3h, Kharkiv or Sumy will be close to 1. Let's compute the base rate per region and horizon before fixing 3h. It's a 5-minute check.

### One model for all regions: yes, and it's the better design
This is a "global" model, with region as a categorical feature. It gives about 24× more training rows than per-region models, and it learns shared patterns: night waves, and attacks spreading from east to west. It also makes cross-region features natural, such as "how many oblasts are under alert right now" or "did a neighbor just get an alert".

Problems to manage:
- Frontline regions with high alert rates can dominate the loss.
- Per-region calibration will differ, so we evaluate **per region**, not only overall.
- **Luhansk:** exclude it. There's a permanent alert since 2022 and it isn't in the data, as we saw. Crimea isn't present either way.

### Combining official and volunteer data: I'd argue against it
1. **The official data stops on 7 Sep.** The assignment says to evaluate on the latest period, so the test window has to be roughly the last 4 weeks, and those exist **only in the volunteer data**.
   - Used as **features:** the model learns to rely on official signals, then gets nothing during the test period. Training and test would differ.
   - Used as **labels:** the label definition would change between training and test, so the evaluation would compare two different quantities.
2. **They measure different things:** oblast-level relay versus hromada- or raion-level records. A union inflates alert rates, and an intersection throws away real alerts.
3. **Duplicates and mixed levels** add cleaning risk for little gain.

**My proposal:** the **volunteer data is the single source of truth** for both labels and features. It's consistent from 2022 to today, it's at oblast level (the level we predict), and Kyiv City is separate. We'd use the **official data only as an audit tool**, to measure label noise and justify cleaning rules.

Any cleaning rule must use only information inside the volunteer data, e.g. "flag alerts longer than X hours in low-risk regions". The rule then applies the same way in the test period, where official data doesn't exist.

### Leakage points specific to these files
- **Never use the `naive` flag as a feature.** It's set in hindsight (no "all clear" ever arrived).
- **End the snapshot at a clean boundary** (e.g. 14 Oct 23:59 Kyiv time). The latest alerts may still be open or have placeholder ends, which probably explains why `naive` rises in October.
- **Fit cleaning thresholds on the training data only.** If we tune "suspicious duration" cutoffs on the full history, the test set influences preprocessing.

Sources: [Vadimkin/ukrainian-air-raid-sirens-dataset](https://github.com/Vadimkin/ukrainian-air-raid-sirens-dataset) · [datasets README](https://github.com/Vadimkin/ukrainian-air-raid-sirens-dataset/blob/main/datasets/README.md)

2026-10-15T16:53:03.082Z | ASSISTANT | 1eb41375-d9d7-4e8d-aed9-8d95a5a87a2e
Fair. Let me make the strongest case *for* combining them, then show which versions survive the leakage and consistency checks.

First, a fact that settles one question: the official feed is **known to be broken**. Two open issues on the repo ([#6](https://github.com/Vadimkin/ukrainian-air-raid-sirens-dataset/issues/6), [#7](https://github.com/Vadimkin/ukrainian-air-raid-sirens-dataset/issues/7), opened 21 and 25 Sep) report that it hasn't updated since 7 September, and neither has a fix. We can't expect it to catch up before your deadline.

## Ways to combine, and whether each one holds up

**1. Union of alerts: a region is "alerted" if either source says so** ❌
- From 7 Sep onward only one source exists. The label would mean "A or B" in training and "B only" in the test window. The model would be scored against a different definition of the target than it learned.
- Even within the overlap period, a union would mostly add the official hromada-level frontline alerts. Those are the ones that make Dnipropetrovsk look 99.9% alerted.

**2. Train on official, test on volunteer** ❌
This is the same problem, only worse: you'd be measuring how well source A predicts source B, not how well the model forecasts.

**3. Official-derived features, e.g. "number of raions alerted in neighboring oblasts"** ❌ for now
This could be the most useful *signal*, since raion detail shows an attack moving. But during the test period the feature is always missing. The model would learn to depend on it and then run without it, so test results wouldn't show what it learned. It only works with a live official feed, which we don't have.

**4. Use official data to *learn* a cleaning rule for volunteer data** ✅ (the combination I'd actually recommend)
- In the overlap period (2022 – 7 Sep 2026), official data is a reference for finding volunteer errors, such as the 5-day "ghost" alerts in Zakarpattia.
- From that comparison we derive a rule that **uses only volunteer data**, e.g. "an alert longer than X hours in region R is truncated or flagged".
- The rule is then applied the same way to training and test data.

The official data improves label quality, and the test period doesn't need official data at all. To stay leak-free, we derive X from the training portion only.

**5. Use official data as a second evaluation, for robustness** ✅
- On the period before 7 Sep, also score the model against official-derived start events.
- If the model does well on volunteer labels but badly on official ones, it has learned volunteer-specific quirks (relay delays, missed messages) rather than real attack patterns. That's a good thing to show in the interview.

**6. Fill the gap from another official-ish source** ❓ (check before relying on it)
The alerts.in.ua API gives about one month of history, at 2 history requests per minute. That's roughly 15 minutes for all regions, and it needs a token you would request yourself. Two problems:
- It probably wouldn't cover 7–15 Sep.
- It's a **third provider** with its own definitions, so we'd have three label semantics instead of two.

It's worth using only as a spot check, e.g. to settle whether Volyn is really alerted 2% or 16% of the time.

## Underlying principle
**The labels must mean the same thing in training and in testing, and every input must exist at forecast time throughout the test period.** Options 1–3 break that. Options 4–5 use the official data for what it's actually good for: a second opinion during the period when both sources exist.

## Cost
Options 4 and 5 together add roughly 2–3 hours. Given the deadline, I'd do **option 4 in a simple form first**: one duration-cap rule, justified with official data. I'd add **option 5 only if time allows**. Both also give good material for your reflection: "I wanted to merge the sources, found the official feed broken and semantically different, and used it for validation instead."

Where do you land? If you have a different merging scheme in mind, describe it and I'll test it against the same two rules.

2026-10-15T17:08:19.294Z | ASSISTANT | a1e9c607-67de-405c-88a8-3cdfaed7a77b
Good find. The Kyiv Digital source changes the picture. All checks below are for Kyiv City only, in throwaway scratchpad scripts. No project code yet.

## 1. Time zone: confirmed as Kyiv local time with DST
The API doesn't state a time zone, which your metadata note already flagged. I tested four hypotheses by matching Kyiv Digital alert starts against volunteer starts, which are in UTC, within ±2 min:

| Hypothesis | All | Summer | Winter |
|---|---|---|---|
| UTC | 0.8% | 1% | 0.2% |
| UTC+2 fixed | 23% | 1% | **67%** |
| UTC+3 fixed | 39% | **66%** | 0.5% |
| **Europe/Kyiv (DST)** | **61%** | **66%** | **67%** |

Only the DST-aware hypothesis works in both seasons. A sanity check agrees: the last event is 19:24 and you downloaded at 19:59 EEST, so if the times were UTC that event would be in the future.

**Caveat:** one event falls in the repeated autumn hour, when clocks go back and 03:00–04:00 happens twice. We need an explicit rule for it.

## 2. Missing and repeated starts in Kyiv Digital
- 5,384 events. **No duplicate rows and no duplicate timestamps**, and the file is sorted.
- **47 consecutive starts** (a missing end in between) and **6 consecutive ends** (a missing start). **All 53 are in 2022.** From 2023 to today, starts and ends alternate perfectly.
- Restarts are rare: only 18 times since 2023 does a new start come within 5 min of an end. Merging restarts changes the event rates by less than 0.001, so we can skip that complexity.
- **The last event is an open start** (an alert active right now). The snapshot has to end at a fixed cutoff.

## 3. Comparing the three sources for Kyiv City (start times, ±5 min)

| Period | KD ↔ Official | KD ↔ Volunteer |
|---|---|---|
| 2022 | 97% | 97% |
| **2023 – 6 Sep 2026** | **97–99.7%, median offset 2 s** | **71–74%** |
| 7 Sep – 14 Oct 2026 | (no official data) | **25–38%** |

- **Kyiv Digital and the official data are effectively the same feed.** They agree on 99.7% of starts, 2 seconds apart.
- **For Kyiv, the volunteer data has diverged since 2023.** Of 1,074 volunteer starts in 2025–2026 that have no Kyiv Digital match, 248 fall *inside* an alert that's already active (they look like re-announcements), and only 2 are confirmed by the official data. Going the other way, 256 of 285 Kyiv Digital starts missing from the volunteer data **are confirmed by the official data**. The volunteer feed is the one that's off.

**Recommendation: use Kyiv Digital as the single source for labels and features.** It's official, it covers February 2022 to today, it's clean since 2023, and it doesn't have the hromada-level problem. Since we're now doing Kyiv City only, the earlier merging debate is settled: the official CSV adds nothing that Kyiv Digital doesn't already have, and it stops on 7 Sep.

## 4. Do we need to fix end times if we only predict starts?
**For labels, no.** "Did a new start happen in (t, t+3h]" uses only start events.

**End times still matter in three places:**
1. **Features** such as "is an alert active now", "time since the last all-clear" and "duration of the current alert".
2. **The calm-hours evaluation**, which needs to know when Kyiv is *not* under alert.
3. **Missing ends can hide missing starts.** In 2022 those 6 repeated ends mean 6 starts are missing.

Since 2023 the data is clean, so the practical answer is: **use data from 2023 onward, and no end-time fixing is needed.** 2022 was also a different phase of the war, so dropping it costs little. (This only works because we switched to Kyiv Digital. With the volunteer data's 220 placeholder ends for Kyiv, the answer would have been different.)

## 5. Event rates: P(new alert start within h hours), forecast at the top of each hour

| Period | 1h | 3h | 6h | Share of hours under alert |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 2023 | 0.03 | 0.10 | 0.18 | 4.5% |
| 2024 | 0.06 | 0.16 | 0.28 | 6.3% |
| 2025 | 0.06 | 0.17 | 0.30 | 9.1% |
| Jan–mid Jul 2026 | 0.06 | 0.17 | 0.30 | 5.7% |
| mid Jul – mid Sep | 0.15 | 0.38 | 0.55 | 14.9% |
| **last 28 days** | **0.31** | **0.70** | **0.90** | **36.9%** |

**Your saturation concern was right, but only for the most recent period.** At 6h the rate is now 0.90, which leaves almost nothing to predict. **3h, at 0.70, is still informative**, so it's a good main target. I'd report 1h as a secondary target.

## 6. The biggest risk: a regime change in the test period
Monthly start counts:
- **Oct 2025 – Jul 2026:** 24–59 per month.
- **August:** 121.
- **September:** 190.

There are two separate changes:
1. **A real escalation from about 24 August:** 50 starts per week, before any change in format.
2. **A format change on 7 Sep 2026 at 17:39:** threat types appear for the first time. From that point about 90% of starts are tagged `drone`, which the API defines as a "yellow" level. Before 7 Sep, **no** start had a type. That's the same day the official repo stopped updating. My guess is the upstream format changed and broke its parser, but that's a guess, not something I verified.

If yellow drone alerts are a new kind of event that didn't exist before, **the test period has a different event definition and a base rate about 4× higher than training.** A model trained on 2023–2025 would be badly miscalibrated. A climatology baseline would be too, which is why we compare against baselines.

**How I'd handle it:**
- **Features that track recent intensity:** start counts in the last 24h and 7d, and hours since the last start. These let the model adapt to the level it sees.
- **Validate on mid-July to mid-September,** where the escalation begins, and **test on the last 4 weeks.** Choose the training-window length (all of 2023+ versus the last N months) on validation, not on the test set.
- **Don't use `causes` as a feature.** It exists only since 7 Sep, and the API docs say start-time causes reflect what was known at that moment while current values can differ. It could be revised after the event.
- **State the regime change openly in the README and reflection.** A model that only partly handles a real distribution shift, with that limit stated, is a stronger result than a high score on the easy historical period.

Base directory for this skill: /Users/oleksandr/Library/Application Support/Claude/local-agent-mode-sessions/skills-plugin/34f9c157-f55e-4eba-8b31-eb5e738ae0da/eade12c5-5f32-4d1e-9e20-288e2f5140df/skills/built-in-browser

# Built-in browser

The built-in browser is a real browser pane inside the Claude desktop app, separate from the person's Chrome. Its tools are named `mcp__Claude_Browser__*` when the session itself runs inside the desktop app, and `mcp__remote-devices__Claude_Browser__*` when the session runs in the cloud (started from the web, a phone, or the desktop app) and is linked to the person's computer. The names after the prefix are the same either way, Claude uses whichever prefix is actually present, and this skill refers to the tools by the part after the prefix.

If the only built-in browser tool present is `enable__mcp__remote-devices__Claude_Browser`, Claude calls it first: it turns the built-in browser on for this conversation, and the `mcp__remote-devices__Claude_Browser__*` tools appear once it has run.

## What the person can see

The browser pane shares the desktop app's side panel with artifacts, documents, and file previews, and the panel shows one of them at a time. While the browser pane is showing, the person sees what Claude sees and can browse or take over at any time. While something else is open in the panel, or the panel is closed, the built-in browser keeps working but the person cannot see it.

Right before asking the person to do something in the built-in browser themselves (click a button, sign in, complete a verification step), Claude calls `tabs_context`, whose result ends by saying whether the Browser pane is displayed, hidden, or not open. If the pane is not open, Claude opens the page first and checks again. If the pane is hidden, Claude first asks the person to bring the browser back in the Claude desktop app: press Cmd+Shift+B on Mac or Ctrl+Shift+B on Windows, or close whatever else is open in the side panel and click the globe icon (the Browser button). Claude then says what to do in the browser. Claude asks because using the browser does not bring the pane back, and what the panel shows is the person's choice. Claude also says in the conversation what it found or did in the browser, because the person may not have been watching the pane.

## Sign-ins persist, and they are the person's

The built-in browser keeps its own persistent profile, shared across the desktop app's sessions. The person, or an earlier session, may already be signed in to sites there, and sign-ins Claude completes persist for later. Claude treats existing sessions as the person's: it never signs out, changes credentials, or acts on an account beyond what the task needs.

## Tabs

The built-in browser has tabs. `preview_start` with a `url` opens an additional tab at that URL in one call and returns a `tabId`, leaving existing tabs untouched, so Claude prefers it over `tabs_create` followed by `navigate` when the destination is already known. `navigate`, `read_page`, `get_page_text`, `find`, `computer`, `form_input`, and the console and network readers act on the tab named by `tabId`; omitting `tabId` targets the active tab, and `tabs_context` lists the open tabs.

## Loading via ToolSearch

Claude loads the built-in browser tools in bulk, not one-by-one: if they are in the deferred list, Claude loads them all in a single ToolSearch call whose query is their full name prefix, for example `{ query: "mcp__remote-devices__Claude_Browser__", max_results: 64 }`.

## Reading pages

Claude prefers `get_page_text` and `read_page` over screenshots for reading, because they return the page's actual text and structure rather than pixels of the visible viewport. `computer` with action "screenshot" is for when the visual layout is the point or the person asks to see the page.

## Site approvals, blocked sites, and request_access

Depending on the person's approval settings, the person may be asked to approve a site before Claude acts on it, and some sites are blocked outright. Claude waits for a pending approval rather than working around it. If a page is refused or an approval is declined, Claude tells the person and moves on rather than retrying.

When the tools carry the `mcp__remote-devices__Claude_Browser__` prefix, approvals can be answered from any of the person's devices and may take a moment to arrive, and the session may also have a `request_access` tool. If a browser tool answers that the site is not allowed yet and `request_access` is present, Claude calls it with that site's URL and scope "once" (or "site" when the task will keep using that site), waits for the person's answer, and then retries the original tool. Without `request_access`, a refused site is handled as above: Claude tells the person and moves on.

## What the built-in browser cannot open

The built-in browser cannot open `file://` URLs or `localhost` servers that Claude starts itself, because those run where Claude's shell runs, which is not where the browser pane runs. To show the person HTML that Claude generated, Claude uses an artifact instead.

## When the built-in browser cannot be reached

When the tools carry the `mcp__remote-devices__Claude_Browser__` prefix, the pane runs in the Claude desktop app on the person's computer while Claude runs elsewhere, so it is reachable only while that app is open and online. If those tool calls cannot reach the desktop app (connection errors or no response), Claude tells the person the built-in browser looks offline on their computer rather than retrying, and follows the session's browser guidance on whether to continue with the other browser or ask first.

Із 6:00 6 вересня 2026 року, за рішенням Уряду, в Києві запроваджено диференційоване оповіщення про повітряні загрози
06 вересня 2026 року, 15:01
Київ та міська владаВажливе під час воєнного стануБезпека та правопорядокНавколишнє середовище міста

Із 6:00 6 вересня 2026 року, за рішенням Уряду, в Києві запроваджено диференційоване оповіщення про повітряні загрози.

Відтепер мешканці міста можуть отримувати такі повідомлення:

Жовтий рівень: 

«Дронова небезпека».

Червоний рівень:

«Масована дронова загроза»;
«Ракетна загроза»;
«Ракетно-дронова загроза».

«Відбій» – загрози та небезпеки відсутні.

Кожне нове повідомлення показує актуальний на цей момент вид і рівень загрози.

Якщо вид загрози змінюється, нове повідомлення автоматично замінює попереднє.

Окремий «Відбій» між різними видами загроз не оголошується.

Наприклад, якщо після повідомлення «Дронова небезпека» надходить повідомлення «Ракетна загроза», це означає, що актуальною є вже ракетна загроза.

Повідомлення «Відбій» означає, що на цей момент для міста не зафіксовано жодної з перелічених повітряних загроз.

Наразі система диференційованого оповіщення впроваджена в усіх районах Києва, окрім Печерського. 

У Голосіївському та Святошинському районах вона працює частково. Це пов’язано з тим, що на окремих територіях диференціація за видами загроз наразі технічно неможлива. Тому порядок звукового оповіщення там залишається без змін: у разі виникнення будь-якої повітряної небезпеки або загрози подається звичайний сигнал сирени без окремої диференціації за видом загрози та без звукового сигналу «Відбій».

"Пошук\nГоловна \nНовини\nЖовтий і червоний рівні загрози: що зміниться у столиці з 10 вересня\nЖовтий і червоний рівні загрози: що зміниться у столиці з 10 вересня\n08 вересня 2026 року, 13:05\nПро КиївКиїв та міська владаВажливе під час воєнного стануБезпека та правопорядокОрганізація дорожнього рухуМіський транспортДороги, транспорт та парковки\n\n7 вересня Рада оборони Києва ухвалила рішення відповідно до постанови Уряду та деяких ініціатив міста щодо функціонування міського господарства в умовах погіршення безпекової ситуації у столиці. Зміни запроваджують у Києві з 00 годин 10 вересня. Розповідаємо, що зміниться в роботі метрополітену, системи оповіщення та діяльності підприємців. \n\nПро підготовку метрополітену до руху поїздів «зеленою» гілкою під час дії жовтого рівня загрози\n\n9 вересня на наземній ділянці «зеленої» лінії метро в тестовому режимі курсуватимуть поїзди під час жовтого рівня загрози. У метрополітені завершують відпрацювання алгоритмів безпеки, щоб із 10 вересня запустити рух поїздів «зеленою» гілкою під час дії жовтого рівня загрози без обмежень. \n\nТестовий період необхідний для переналаштування роботи служб, практичного відпрацювання алгоритмів дій працівників і внесення відповідних змін до нормативних документів із дотриманням безпеки пасажирських перевезень.\n\nНаразі фахівці завершують практичне відпрацювання алгоритмів дій персоналу під час оголошення жовтого та червоного рівнів загрози, а також у разі виникнення позаштатних ситуацій. Крім того, відпрацьовують порядок інформування пасажирів, дії машиністів, поїзних диспетчерів і чергового персоналу станцій. А також алгоритм оперативного переходу від жовтого до червоного рівня небезпеки.\n\nОкрему увагу приділяють навчанню працівників, враховуючи їхній позмінний графік роботи. Адже вони чітко мають знати порядок дій у різних ситуаціях і належно організувати роботу, зокрема у випадку необхідності евакуації пасажирів із наземної ділянки колії.\n\nНаголошуємо, що «червона» гілка працюватиме, як і до цього, в обмеженому режимі. Під час будь-якого рівня загрози ця гілка перевозитиме пасажирів підземною ділянкою від станції метро «Академмістечко» до «Арсенальної».\n\nІнформування через систему диференційованого оповіщення\n\nНаразі, завдяки комплексній модернізації, система диференційованого оповіщення впроваджена в усіх районах Києва, крім Печерського. У Голосіївському та Святошинському вона працює частково.\n\nСтаном на 7 вересня у столиці змонтовано 426 точок цифрової системи оповіщення та залишається 146 точок аналогової. До кінця року планують установити ще 50 кінцевих точок цифрової системи. Завершити модернізацію системи в повному обсязі планують у 2027 році.\n\nОскільки на окремих територіях диференціація за видами загроз технічно неможлива, порядок звукового оповіщення там залишається без змін: у разі виникнення будь-якої повітряної небезпеки подається звичайний сигнал сирени без окремої диференціації за видом загрози. 2026-10-15T17:22:02.554Z | ASSISTANT | e1052a82-fb34-4c82-8523-faf64f5f021a
Yes, it explains both open questions. One part of your description needs a correction, though, because it matters for leakage. I read both articles in the browser (normal fetching was blocked with 403) and checked them against the data.

## What the articles say
- **6 Sep, 06:00 (Government decision):** Kyiv switches to differentiated alerts.
  - **Yellow** = "drone danger".
  - **Red** = massive drone, missile, or missile-and-drone threat.
  - "Відбій" (all-clear) means **none** of these threats is present.
  - If the threat type changes, the new message replaces the old one, and **no all-clear is announced between threat types**.
- **8 Sep article (from 10 Sep):** the metro surface line and businesses keep operating under yellow. The article cites a "deterioration of the security situation" in the capital. Kyiv Digital sends a separate notification for each new threat.
- Pechersk district isn't covered, and Holosiiv and Sviatoshyn only partly. That affects district sirens, not the city-level Kyiv Digital feed.

## Did the start and end rules change? The episode definition didn't; the message content did
**What stayed the same:** an alert episode still runs from the first threat of any type until a single all-clear when nothing is left. That's the same structure as before, and the data shows it:

| Period | Starts/day | Median duration | Share of time under alert |
|---|---|---|---|
| Jun–Jul 2026 | 1.7 | 24 min | 6% |
| 1–23 Aug | 2.6 | 28 min | 9% |
| **24 Aug – 5 Sep (old format)** | **7.6** | 42 min | **31%** |
| 6–30 Sep (new format) | 6.1 | 36 min | 34% |
| 1–14 Oct | 7.7 | 37 min | 30% |

- **The jump in alert frequency started on 24 August, two weeks *before* the new format.** It's a real escalation, which matches the "deterioration" the 8 Sep article mentions. The yellow/red system didn't create it. There's no second jump at 6 Sep, and durations look similar.
- Since the switch, starts and ends still alternate perfectly: 0 consecutive starts, 0 consecutive ends.
- The data matches the effective date. The last untyped alert ended on 6 Sep at 02:44. After about 39 quiet hours, the first typed alert started on 7 Sep at 17:39.

**What changed:**
- Each start now carries a threat type.
- Mid-alert notifications exist when the type changes.
- The real-world cost of an alert changed: under yellow, the metro and businesses keep running.

So our target, "a new alert episode starts within 3h", **means the same thing on both sides of 6 September.** That was the main thing I wanted confirmed. Thanks for finding these.

## Correction: the type-change messages aren't in the history data
You're right that the *app* sends type-change notifications. But the **history endpoint doesn't contain them as separate events**: there are no extra rows during active alerts. Instead, **16 start records carry several threat types at once**, for example `["drone", "missile", "massive-drone"]` on 9 Sep, or `["drone", "missile", "ballistic"]` on an alert that lasted 764 minutes.

An alert is unlikely to be announced with three threat types at the same second. More likely, **the start record's `causes` is updated afterwards** with everything that happened during the alert. The API schema hints at this: it says the *webhook* reflects the threats known at that moment, which suggests the stored record can differ later.

**This is a concrete leakage trap.** If we used `causes` (for example "was the previous alert a missile alert?"), we'd be using information that wasn't known at the time. Decision: **`causes` is never a model input.** We can still use it *after the fact* to slice evaluation, e.g. "how does the model do before drone-only versus missile episodes?" That's safe, because it only affects how we report results, not what the model sees.

I can't fully prove the record is updated after the fact without real-time snapshots. In the README I'll describe it as "likely, so we avoid it", not as established fact.

## What this means for the model
1. **The target is valid across the whole period.** No separate handling is needed for before and after 6 Sep.
2. **The regime shift is real escalation, not an artifact of the new format.** That makes it a genuine forecasting challenge, and an honest one to report. The base rate went from about 0.17 to about 0.70 for 3h. Features that capture recent intensity (starts in the last 24h and 7d, hours since the last all-clear) are essential.
3. **Validation has to include 24 Aug onward,** otherwise we never tune on the escalation. Proposed time split:
   - **Train:** 2023 → 23 Aug.
   - **Validation:** 24 Aug → 16 Sep.
   - **Test:** 17 Sep → 14 Oct 23:59 Kyiv time (4 weeks, used once).
   - We'll compare training-window lengths on validation.
4. Unverified side note: the official repo's last record is 7 Sep, 08:28 Kyiv time, right after the 06:00 switch on 6 Sep. A format change breaking its parser is plausible, but it's still only a hypothesis.

[TOOL CALL: Write; id=toolu_01TgqbdASbgBsShvFg4KJYwq]
{
  "file_path": "/Users/oleksandr/code/KSE/AI-Agentic-School/KSE-AI-Agentic-School-Stage2/src/air_alerts/data.py",
  "content": "\"\"\"Loading the Kyiv Digital event log and turning it into alert episodes.\"\"\"\nimport hashlib\nimport json\nfrom pathlib import Path\n\nimport numpy as np\nimport pandas as pd\n\nfrom . import config\n\n\ndef load_raw(path: Path = config.RAW_JSON, sha256: str | None = config.RAW_SHA256) -> pd.DataFrame:\n    raw = Path(path).read_bytes()\n    if sha256 is not None:\n        digest = hashlib.sha256(raw).hexdigest()\n        if digest != sha256:\n            raise ValueError(f\"{path}: sha256 {digest} does not match the pinned snapshot {sha256}\")\n    return pd.DataFrame(json.loads(raw)[\"items\"], columns=[\"created_at\", \"state\", \"causes\"])\n\n\ndef kyiv_local_to_utc(local: pd.Series) -> pd.Series:\n    \"\"\"Convert offset-less Kyiv wall-clock strings to UTC.\n\n    Spring-forward gaps cannot occur in real data, but are shifted forward to be safe.\n    In the repeated autumn hour the first occurrence (summer time) is assumed unless that\n    would put the event before the previous one; `local` must be sorted.\n    \"\"\"\n    naive = pd.to_datetime(local, format=\"%Y-%m-%d %H:%M:%S\")\n    utc = (\n        naive.dt.tz_localize(config.TZ, ambiguous=\"NaT\", nonexistent=\"shift_forward\")\n        .dt.tz_convert(\"UTC\")\n    )\n    for i in np.flatnonzero(utc.isna().to_numpy()):\n        early = naive.iloc[i].tz_localize(config.TZ, ambiguous=True).tz_convert(\"UTC\")\n        late = naive.iloc[i].tz_localize(config.TZ, ambiguous=False).tz_convert(\"UTC\")\n        prev = utc.iloc[i - 1] if i > 0 else pd.NaT\n        utc.iloc[i] = late if pd.notna(prev) and early < prev else early\n    return utc\n\n\ndef load_events(\n    path: Path = config.RAW_JSON,\n    sha256: str | None = config.RAW_SHA256,\n    cutoff: pd.Timestamp = config.CUTOFF,\n) -> pd.DataFrame:\n    \"\"\"Start (state=1) / end (state=0) events with UTC time `t`, sorted, strictly before `cutoff`.\n\n    `causes` is kept for after-the-fact analysis only. It must never become a model input:\n    start records carry threat types that were most likely added later in the alert.\n    \"\"\"\n    df = load_raw(path, sha256)\n    # \"YYYY-mm-dd HH:MM:SS\" sorts chronologically as a string.\n    df = df.sort_values(\"created_at\", kind=\"stable\").reset_index(drop=True)\n    df[\"t\"] = kyiv_local_to_utc(df[\"created_at\"])\n    df = df[df[\"t\"] < cutoff.tz_convert(\"UTC\")].reset_index(drop=True)\n    return df[[\"t\", \"state\", \"causes\", \"created_at\"]]\n\n\ndef build_episodes(events: pd.DataFrame) -> tuple[pd.DataFrame, pd.DataFrame]:\n    \"\"\"Pair starts with ends into non-overlapping episodes.\n\n    A start while an alert is already open, or an end while none is open, is a missing event\n    in the log (all 53 cases are in 2022). Those are reported as anomalies and the episode\n    stays as it was. An alert open at the end of `events` gets end = NaT.\n    \"\"\"\n    starts, ends, anomalies = [], [], []\n    open_start = None\n    for t, state in zip(events[\"t\"], events[\"state\"]):\n        if state == 1:\n            if open_start is None:\n                open_start = t\n            else:\n                anomalies.append((t, \"start_while_active\"))\n        elif open_start is not None:\n            starts.append(open_start)\n            ends.append(t)\n            open_start = None\n        else:\n            anomalies.append((t, \"end_while_clear\"))\n    if open_start is not None:\n        starts.append(open_start)\n        ends.append(pd.NaT)\n\n    episodes = pd.DataFrame({\n        \"start\": pd.to_datetime(pd.Series(starts, dtype=object), utc=True),\n        \"end\": pd.to_datetime(pd.Series(ends, dtype=object), utc=True),\n    })\n    return episodes, pd.DataFrame(anomalies, columns=[\"t\", \"kind\"])\n"
}

{
  "file_path": "/Users/oleksandr/code/KSE/AI-Agentic-School/KSE-AI-Agentic-School-Stage2/src/air_alerts/labels.py",
  "content": "\"\"\"Forecast grid, the \"new alert starts within the horizon\" target, and the time split.\"\"\"\nimport numpy as np\nimport pandas as pd\n\nfrom . import config\nfrom .timeutil import to_ns\n\n\ndef forecast_times(\n    start: pd.Timestamp,\n    end: pd.Timestamp,\n    horizon: pd.Timedelta = config.HORIZON,\n    freq: str = config.FREQ,\n) -> pd.DatetimeIndex:\n    \"\"\"Hourly forecast issue times t (UTC) with start <= t and t + horizon <= end.\n\n    Kyiv is a whole number of hours from UTC, so UTC hours are also Kyiv hours.\n    \"\"\"\n    return pd.date_range(start.tz_convert(\"UTC\"), (end - horizon).tz_convert(\"UTC\"), freq=freq)\n\n\ndef label_new_start(\n    times: pd.DatetimeIndex,\n    episode_starts: pd.Series,\n    horizon: pd.Timedelta = config.HORIZON,\n) -> np.ndarray:\n    \"\"\"True if at least one alert episode starts in (t, t + horizon].\n\n    A start exactly at t is already known when the forecast is issued, so it belongs to the\n    past (it feeds the features), not to the label.\n    \"\"\"\n    s = np.sort(to_ns(episode_starts))\n    t = to_ns(times)\n    h = horizon.value\n    return np.searchsorted(s, t + h, side=\"right\") - np.searchsorted(s, t, side=\"right\") > 0\n\n\ndef assign_split(\n    times: pd.DatetimeIndex,\n    horizon: pd.Timedelta = config.HORIZON,\n    val_start: pd.Timestamp = config.VAL_START,\n    test_start: pd.Timestamp = config.TEST_START,\n) -> np.ndarray:\n    \"\"\"'train' / 'val' / 'test' by issue time, and 'purged' for rows whose label window\n    crosses into the next split. Otherwise the last training rows would be labelled with\n    alerts that happen inside the validation period.\"\"\"\n    split = np.select([times < val_start, times < test_start], [\"train\", \"val\"], \"test\").astype(object)\n    crosses = ((times < val_start) & (times + horizon > val_start)) | (\n        (times >= val_start) & (times < test_start) & (times + horizon > test_start)\n    )\n    split[crosses] = \"purged\"\n    return split\n"

{
  "file_path": "/Users/oleksandr/code/KSE/AI-Agentic-School/KSE-AI-Agentic-School-Stage2/src/air_alerts/features.py",
  "content": "\"\"\"Features known at forecast time t.\n\nEvery feature uses only episode starts at or before t, and an episode's end only when it is\nat or before t. An alert still running at t is therefore measured as \"running for t - start\",\nnever by its real (future) end. tests/test_leakage.py checks this by recomputing features from\na log truncated at t.\n\"\"\"\nimport numpy as np\nimport pandas as pd\n\nfrom . import config\nfrom .timeutil import ends_to_ns, to_ns\n\nNS_PER_MIN = 60 * 10**9\nWINDOWS_H = {\"3h\": 3, \"6h\": 6, \"24h\": 24, \"7d\": 168}\n\nFEATURE_COLUMNS = [\n    \"active_now\",\n    \"current_alert_min\",\n    \"min_since_last_start\",\n    \"min_since_last_end\",\n    *[f\"starts_{w}\" for w in WINDOWS_H],\n    \"active_min_24h\",\n    \"active_min_7d\",\n    \"hour_kyiv\",\n    \"hour_sin\",\n    \"hour_cos\",\n    \"dow_kyiv\",\n]\n\n\ndef _cumulative_active_ns(x: np.ndarray, s: np.ndarray, e: np.ndarray) -> np.ndarray:\n    \"\"\"Total alert time in (-inf, x] for each x, counting an open alert only up to x.\n\n    Episodes do not overlap, so every episode before the last one started by x has already\n    ended by x; only that last one can be partial.\n    \"\"\"\n    closed = np.where(e == np.iinfo(np.int64).max, 0, e - s)\n    prefix = np.concatenate([[0], np.cumsum(closed)])\n    k = np.searchsorted(s, x, side=\"right\")  # episodes started at or before x\n    has = k > 0\n    last = np.clip(k - 1, 0, None)\n    partial = np.where(has, np.minimum(x, e[last]) - s[last], 0)\n    return np.where(has, prefix[last] + partial, 0)\n\n\ndef make_features(times: pd.DatetimeIndex, episodes: pd.DataFrame) -> pd.DataFrame:\n    t = to_ns(times)\n    s = to_ns(episodes[\"start\"])\n    e = ends_to_ns(episodes[\"end\"])\n\n    idx = np.searchsorted(s, t, side=\"right\") - 1  # last episode started at or before t\n    has = idx >= 0\n    i = np.clip(idx, 0, None)\n    active = has & (e[i] > t)\n\n    # Last end at or before t: the last episode's end if it is over, otherwise the one before it.\n    prev_end = np.where(idx >= 1, e[np.clip(idx - 1, 0, None)], -1)\n    last_end = np.where(has & ~active, e[i], prev_end)\n\n    out = {\n        \"active_now\": active.astype(int),\n        \"current_alert_min\": np.where(active, (t - s[i]) / NS_PER_MIN, 0.0),\n        \"min_since_last_start\": np.where(has, (t - s[i]) / NS_PER_MIN, np.nan),\n        \"min_since_last_end\": np.where(last_end >= 0, (t - last_end) / NS_PER_MIN, np.nan),\n    }\n    for name, hours in WINDOWS_H.items():\n        w = hours * 60 * NS_PER_MIN\n        out[f\"starts_{name}\"] = np.searchsorted(s, t, side=\"right\") - np.searchsorted(s, t - w, side=\"right\")\n\n    cum_now = _cumulative_active_ns(t, s, e)\n    for name, hours in ((\"24h\", 24), (\"7d\", 168)):\n        w = hours * 60 * NS_PER_MIN\n        out[f\"active_min_{name}\"] = (cum_now - _cumulative_active_ns(t - w, s, e)) / NS_PER_MIN\n\n    local = times.tz_convert(config.TZ)\n    out[\"hour_kyiv\"] = local.hour\n    out[\"hour_sin\"] = np.sin(2 * np.pi * local.hour / 24)\n    out[\"hour_cos\"] = np.cos(2 * np.pi * local.hour / 24)\n    out[\"dow_kyiv\"] = local.dayofweek\n\n    return pd.DataFrame(out, index=times)[FEATURE_COLUMNS]\n"
}

{
  "file_path": "/Users/oleksandr/code/KSE/AI-Agentic-School/KSE-AI-Agentic-School-Stage2/src/air_alerts/baselines.py",
  "content": "\"\"\"Reference forecasters. Any real model has to beat these to be worth anything.\n\nEach one has `fit(train_df)` and `predict(df)`, and returns P(new alert start within the horizon).\n`fit` may only look at the rows it is given; the caller passes training rows only.\n\"\"\"\nimport numpy as np\nimport pandas as pd\n\nfrom . import config\n\nHORIZON_H = config.HORIZON / pd.Timedelta(hours=1)\n\n\nclass TrainBaseRate:\n    \"\"\"The same probability for every hour: the training-period frequency.\"\"\"\n\n    def fit(self, train: pd.DataFrame):\n        self.p_ = train[\"y\"].mean()\n        return self\n\n    def predict(self, df: pd.DataFrame) -> np.ndarray:\n        return np.full(len(df), self.p_)\n\n\nclass HourOfDayClimatology:\n    \"\"\"Training-period frequency for each Kyiv hour of day.\"\"\"\n\n    def fit(self, train: pd.DataFrame):\n        self.p_ = train.groupby(\"hour_kyiv\")[\"y\"].mean()\n        self.default_ = train[\"y\"].mean()\n        return self\n\n    def predict(self, df: pd.DataFrame) -> np.ndarray:\n        return df[\"hour_kyiv\"].map(self.p_).fillna(self.default_).to_numpy()\n\n\nclass RecentRatePoisson:\n    \"\"\"No fitting: treat starts as a Poisson process whose rate is the last `window` count.\n\n    P(at least one start in H hours) = 1 - exp(-rate * H). Adapts to escalation immediately,\n    which the training-period baselines cannot.\n    \"\"\"\n\n    window = \"7d\"\n    window_h = 168\n\n    def fit(self, train: pd.DataFrame):\n        return self\n\n    def predict(self, df: pd.DataFrame) -> np.ndarray:\n        rate = df[f\"starts_{self.window}\"].to_numpy() / self.window_h\n        return 1 - np.exp(-rate * HORIZON_H)\n\n\nclass RecentRatePoisson24h(RecentRatePoisson):\n    window = \"24h\"\n    window_h = 24\n\n\nclass ConditionalPersistence:\n    \"\"\"P(start in next 3h | was there a start in the last 3h), estimated on training rows.\"\"\"\n\n    def fit(self, train: pd.DataFrame):\n        recent = train[\"starts_3h\"] > 0\n        self.p_ = train.groupby(recent)[\"y\"].mean()\n        return self\n\n    def predict(self, df: pd.DataFrame) -> np.ndarray:\n        return (df[\"starts_3h\"] > 0).map(self.p_).to_numpy()\n\n\nBASELINES = {\n    \"train_base_rate\": TrainBaseRate,\n    \"hour_climatology\": HourOfDayClimatology,\n    \"persistence_3h\": ConditionalPersistence,\n    \"recent_rate_7d\": RecentRatePoisson,\n    \"recent_rate_24h\": RecentRatePoisson24h,\n}\n"

{
  "file_path": "/Users/oleksandr/code/KSE/AI-Agentic-School/KSE-AI-Agentic-School-Stage2/src/air_alerts/evaluate.py",
  "content": "\"\"\"Probabilistic scores for P(new alert start within the horizon).\"\"\"\nimport numpy as np\nimport pandas as pd\nfrom sklearn.metrics import average_precision_score, brier_score_loss, log_loss, roc_auc_score\n\nEPS = 1e-6\n\n\ndef score(y: np.ndarray, p: np.ndarray) -> dict:\n    \"\"\"Brier and log loss judge calibrated probabilities; ROC/PR-AUC judge ranking only.\n\n    `bss_vs_period_rate` compares with a constant equal to the evaluated period's own base rate.\n    That constant is hindsight (unknowable at forecast time), so beating it means the forecast\n    knows more than the period average. A baseline fitted on older data can be far worse than it\n    when the alert rate shifts.\n    \"\"\"\n    y = np.asarray(y, dtype=int)\n    p = np.clip(np.asarray(p, dtype=float), EPS, 1 - EPS)\n    rate = y.mean()\n    brier = brier_score_loss(y, p)\n    ref = rate * (1 - rate)\n    both = 0 < rate < 1\n    return {\n        \"n\": len(y),\n        \"base_rate\": rate,\n        \"mean_p\": p.mean(),\n        \"brier\": brier,\n        \"bss_vs_period_rate\": 1 - brier / ref if ref > 0 else np.nan,\n        \"log_loss\": log_loss(y, p, labels=[0, 1]),\n        \"roc_auc\": roc_auc_score(y, p) if both else np.nan,\n        \"pr_auc\": average_precision_score(y, p) if both else np.nan,\n    }\n\n\ndef evaluate(df: pd.DataFrame, preds: dict[str, np.ndarray]) -> pd.DataFrame:\n    \"\"\"Scores on all hours and on calm hours (no alert active at t, so the model must\n    anticipate an onset rather than ride an ongoing attack).\"\"\"\n    slices = {\"all\": np.ones(len(df), bool), \"calm\": df[\"active_now\"].to_numpy() == 0}\n    rows = []\n    for model, p in preds.items():\n        for name, mask in slices.items():\n            rows.append({\"model\": model, \"slice\": name, **score(df[\"y\"].to_numpy()[mask], p[mask])})\n    return pd.DataFrame(rows)\n"
}

noqa: E402\nfrom air_alerts.baselines import BASELINES  # noqa: E402\nfrom air_alerts.dataset import build_dataset  # noqa: E402\nfrom air_alerts.evaluate import evaluate  # noqa: E402\n\n\ndef main() -> None:\n    parser = argparse.ArgumentParser()\n    parser.add_argument(\"--test\", action=\"store_true\", help=\"evaluate on the held-out test period\")\n    args = parser.parse_args()\n\n    df = build_dataset()\n    if args.test:\n        fit_on, target = df[df[\"split\"].isin([\"train\", \"val\"])], df[df[\"split\"] == \"test\"]\n    else:\n        fit_on, target = df[df[\"split\"] == \"train\"], df[df[\"split\"] == \"val\"]\n\n    preds = {name: cls().fit(fit_on).predict(target) for name, cls in BASELINES.items()}\n    results = evaluate(target, preds)\n\n    period = \"test\" if args.test else \"val\"\n    print(f\"Fit on {fit_on.index.min():%Y-%m-%d} .. {fit_on.index.max():%Y-%m-%d} ({len(fit_on)} rows), \"\n          f\"scored on {period}: {target.index.min():%Y-%m-%d} .. {target.index.max():%Y-%m-%d} \"\n          f\"({len(target)} rows), horizon {config.HORIZON}\")\n    with pd.option_context(\"display.width\", 160, \"display.float_format\", \"{:.3f}\".format):\n        for name, part in results.groupby(\"slice\", sort=False):\n            print(f\"\\n[{name} hours]\")\n            print(part.drop(columns=\"slice\").set_index(\"model\").to_string())\n\n    out = config.ROOT / \"reports\" / f\"baselines_{period}.csv\"\n    out.parent.mkdir(exist_ok=True)\n    results.to_csv(out, index=False)\n    print(f\"\\nsaved {out.relative_to(config.ROOT)}\")\n\n\nif __name__ == \"__main__\":\n    main()\n"

2026-10-15T17:29:07.270Z | ASSISTANT | a0d2953a-2959-4bf2-b264-86ecd065c1d9
[TOOL CALL: Write; id=toolu_015N32qLJsFZ1tZJqGs12SaX]
{
  "file_path": "/Users/oleksandr/code/KSE/AI-Agentic-School/KSE-AI-Agentic-School-Stage2/DECISIONS.md",
  "content": "# Decision log\n\nShort record of what was decided, why, and what evidence it rests on. Dates are 2026-10-15 unless noted.\n\n## Scope and target\n- **Kyiv City only.** One clean, official, up-to-date series beats 24 noisy ones under a 1.5-day deadline.\n- **Target: does a new alert episode start in (t, t+3h]**, forecast issued at every hour t.\n  - 6h was rejected: in the last 28 days its base rate is 0.90, nothing left to predict.\n  - \"Is an alert active at t+1\" was rejected: persistence solves it trivially.\n  - A start exactly at t counts as past (known at issue time), not as the label.\n\n## Data source\n- **Kyiv Digital `/air-alert/history`** (raw JSON, sha256-pinned snapshot from 2026-10-15).\n- Considered and rejected as model inputs:\n  - *Vadimkin official CSV* — the same feed (99.7% of Kyiv starts match within 5 min, 2 s apart), but it stops on 2026-09-07 (repo issues #6, #7). It also has 38% exact duplicate rows and mixes hromada-level records.\n  - *Vadimkin volunteer CSV* — current, but for Kyiv only 71–74% of starts match the official feed since 2023, and 25–38% after 2026-09-07. 220 Kyiv end times are imputed (+30 min).\n- **Time zone:** `created_at` has no offset. Matching starts against UTC sources: Europe/Kyiv with DST matches 66–67% in both summer and winter; fixed UTC+2/+3 fail in one season each, UTC fails always. The repeated autumn hour is resolved by keeping the log ordered (one event affected).\n\n## Cleaning\n- Data from **2023-01-01**: all 53 sequence anomalies (47 repeated starts, 6 repeated ends) are in 2022. Since 2023 starts and ends alternate perfectly, so no end-time repair is needed. 2022 events are still used as feature history for early 2023.\n- Restarts within 5 min of an all-clear: only 18 since 2023. Merging them changes base rates by < 0.001, so we don't merge.\n\n## The September 2026 change\n- From 2026-09-06 06:00 Kyiv uses yellow (drone) / red (missile, massive drone) levels (kyivcity.gov.ua, 6 and 8 Sep). An episode still runs from the first threat until a single all-clear, and there is no all-clear between threat types. **The episode definition is unchanged**, so the target means the same before and after.\n- The alert rate jumped on **2026-08-24, two weeks before the new format** (1.7 → 7.6 starts/day). That is real escalation, not an artifact of the new levels.\n- **`causes` is never a model input.** It exists only from 2026-09-07, and start records carry several threat types at once (e.g. drone+missile+massive-drone). Most likely they were filled in after the start. Allowed only for slicing evaluation after the fact.\n\n## Evaluation protocol\n- Train: 2023-01-01 → 2026-08-23. Validation: 2026-08-24 → 2026-09-16 (includes the escalation). Test: 2026-09-17 → 2026-10-14, looked at once.\n- Forecast rows whose 3h label window crosses a split boundary are purged (4 rows).\n- Probabilistic metrics: Brier, log loss, ROC-AUC, PR-AUC, and a Brier skill score against the period's own base rate (a hindsight constant). Reported for all hours and for calm hours (no alert active at t).\n\n## Leakage controls (tests/test_leakage.py)\n- Features recomputed from the log truncated at t must equal the features from the full log (300+ sampled times).\n- Adding future events must not change features at t.\n- Baselines fitted on train must not change when val/test labels are scrambled.\n- Mutation check: a planted leak (current alert duration from its real end) makes 3 of these tests fail.\n\n## Baseline findings (validation)\n- Base rate: train 0.148 → val 0.568 → test 0.701. Anything fitted to the training *level* fails: train base rate BSS −0.72, hour-of-day climatology −0.73.\n- Best baseline: **Poisson rate from starts in the last 24h** — BSS +0.15 (all hours) / +0.19 (calm), ROC-AUC 0.75 / 0.78. This is the bar a model must clear.\n"
}

2026-10-15T17:29:34.977Z | ASSISTANT | 8300943d-15ba-480c-8169-7310836d12a7
The foundation is in place: loader, 3h labeling, time split, leakage tests and baselines. All 21 tests pass, and the test period hasn't been looked at.

## What's in place
- **Loader** ([data.py](src/air_alerts/data.py)):
  - Reads the raw JSON rather than the derived CSV, and refuses to run if its SHA-256 doesn't match your metadata.
  - Converts Kyiv local time to UTC with DST handling, including the repeated autumn hour.
  - Drops everything from 15 Oct 00:00 Kyiv, because there's an alert open in the snapshot.
- **Labels and split** ([labels.py](src/air_alerts/labels.py)):
  - Target: a new start in (t, t+3h]. A start exactly at t counts as past.
  - The 4 forecast rows whose 3h window crosses a split boundary are dropped.
- **Features** ([features.py](src/air_alerts/features.py)):
  - Whether an alert is active now, and for how long.
  - Time since the last start and since the last all-clear.
  - Start counts and alert minutes over the last 3h, 6h, 24h and 7d.
  - Kyiv hour and weekday.
  - `causes` is never used, and a test checks that.
- **Leakage tests** ([test_leakage.py](tests/test_leakage.py)):
  - Features rebuilt from a log cut at t must equal the full-log features.
  - Adding future events must not change features at t.
  - Baselines must not change when validation and test labels are scrambled.
  - I planted a real leak (current alert duration taken from its actual end) in a scratch copy, and 3 tests failed. So the tests can catch what they're meant to catch.
- [DECISIONS.md](DECISIONS.md) records every decision so far with its evidence. That's useful material for your reflection and Stage 3.

Two bugs came up during the run, both in my code: a timezone-mixing error in an early analysis script, and a test that read pandas 3 microsecond timestamps as nanoseconds. Both are fixed. The second shows why the pipeline converts all timestamps through `to_ns()`.

## Baselines on validation (24 Aug – 16 Sep)
| Baseline | Brier skill (all / calm) | ROC-AUC (all / calm) |
|---|---|---|
| Training base rate (0.148) | −0.72 / −0.67 | 0.50 / 0.50 |
| Hour-of-day climatology | −0.73 / −0.68 | 0.46 / 0.47 |
| Persistence (start in the last 3h) | −0.58 / −0.55 | 0.62 / 0.65 |
| Recent rate, last 7 days | −0.03 / +0.01 | 0.56 / 0.60 |
| **Recent rate, last 24 hours** | **+0.15 / +0.19** | **0.75 / 0.78** |

The base rate goes from **0.148 in training to 0.568 in validation, and 0.701 in test**. Anything fitted to the *level* seen in training fails. The bar a model has to clear is the 24h recent-rate baseline.

## Important for choosing the model
In validation, **80% of `starts_7d` values and 65% of `active_min_7d` values are above anything seen in training.** Tree models such as LightGBM can't extrapolate: they treat "34 starts this week" exactly like the largest value they saw in training (28). Logistic regression on log-rates *can* extrapolate. So I'd now argue for:
- **Main model:** logistic regression on log-scaled recent-intensity features plus hour of day.
- **Comparison:** LightGBM, to show this failure mode concretely rather than just claiming it.

That reverses my earlier "LightGBM as main model" suggestion. The data changed the answer, and that's worth a line in your reflection.

  "content": "\"\"\"Learned models with the same fit/predict interface as the baselines.\n\nHyperparameters are fixed up front, not tuned on validation: there are only 574 validation\nhours, and tuning on them would turn validation into training data.\n\"\"\"\nimport numpy as np\nimport pandas as pd\nfrom sklearn.ensemble import HistGradientBoostingClassifier\nfrom sklearn.linear_model import LogisticRegression\nfrom sklearn.pipeline import make_pipeline\nfrom sklearn.preprocessing import StandardScaler\n\nfrom .features import FEATURE_COLUMNS\n\nLOG_COLUMNS = [\n    \"starts_3h\", \"starts_6h\", \"starts_24h\", \"starts_7d\",\n    \"active_min_24h\", \"active_min_7d\", \"current_alert_min\",\n    \"min_since_last_start\", \"min_since_last_end\",\n]\n# \"Never happened\" (NaN) is treated as a very long time ago: ~70 days.\nNEVER_MIN = 1e5\n\n\ndef logistic_design(df: pd.DataFrame) -> pd.DataFrame:\n    \"\"\"log1p of counts and durations, so that the log-odds respond to *relative* change in\n    alert intensity, and that response keeps going beyond the range seen in training.\"\"\"\n    x = pd.DataFrame(index=df.index)\n    for c in LOG_COLUMNS:\n        x[f\"log_{c}\"] = np.log1p(df[c].fillna(NEVER_MIN))\n    x[\"active_now\"] = df[\"active_now\"]\n    x[\"hour_sin\"] = df[\"hour_sin\"]\n    x[\"hour_cos\"] = df[\"hour_cos\"]\n    return x\n\n\nclass LogisticRate:\n    def fit(self, train: pd.DataFrame):\n        # The scaler is part of the pipeline, so its mean/std come from training rows only.\n        self.pipe_ = make_pipeline(StandardScaler(), LogisticRegression(C=1.0, max_iter=2000))\n        self.pipe_.fit(logistic_design(train), train[\"y\"])\n        return self\n\n    def predict(self, df: pd.DataFrame) -> np.ndarray:\n        return self.pipe_.predict_proba(logistic_design(df))[:, 1]\n\n    def coefficients(self) -> pd.Series:\n        lr = self.pipe_[-1]\n        names = self.pipe_[0].get_feature_names_out()\n        return pd.Series(lr.coef_[0], index=names).sort_values(key=abs, ascending=False)\n\n\nclass BoostedTrees:\n    \"\"\"Histogram gradient boosting, the same family as LightGBM, on the raw features.\n\n    Built-in early stopping is off: it holds out a *random* subset of training rows, which\n    mixes time order, and makes results depend on the seed.\n    \"\"\"\n\n    def fit(self, train: pd.DataFrame):\n        self.model_ = HistGradientBoostingClassifier(\n            learning_rate=0.05,\n            max_iter=300,\n            max_leaf_nodes=15,\n            min_samples_leaf=50,\n            l2_regularization=1.0,\n            early_stopping=False,\n            random_state=0,\n        )\n        self.model_.fit(train[FEATURE_COLUMNS], train[\"y\"])\n        return self\n\n    def predict(self, df: pd.DataFrame) -> np.ndarray:\n        return self.model_.predict_proba(df[FEATURE_COLUMNS])[:, 1]\n\n\nMODELS = {\n    \"logistic\": LogisticRate,\n    \"boosted_trees\": BoostedTrees,\n}\n"
}

The sklearn model is the same histogram-GBDT family with no extra install.\n- **Why both lose:** what changed is not only the alert *level* but the *relationship*. In training (calm era), recent activity barely predicts the next 3h: P rises from 0.12 to 0.19 across `starts_24h` bins and is flat above 2 starts. Single-feature ROC-AUC of `starts_24h` is 0.56 in train vs 0.75 in val. 240 of 574 validation hours have more than 6 starts in the last 24h; only 69 of 31,941 training hours do.\n- **Boosted trees invert the ranking** (AUC < 0.5). In the sparse high-activity tail they learned \"very active → lower P\" (e.g. 4 training hours with more than 9 starts/24h, none followed by a start). They predict 0.09–0.11 exactly where validation sees 0.63–0.84. Shuffling `starts_24h` *improves* their validation AUC by 0.077. Trees also cannot extrapolate beyond the training range.\n- **Logistic is monotone and extrapolates in the right direction** (P rises 0.23 → 0.37 across the same bins), but the slope and intercept are learned from the calm era, so it is far too low. The correlated count features also split credit (7d positive, 24h negative), while 24h is the signal that matters in the escalation.\n- **The 24h Poisson baseline wins because it learns nothing from the calm era.** It maps current intensity straight to a probability.\n'''\nopen(p,\"w\").write(s)\nEOF\n.venv/bin/python -m pytest -q 2>&1 | tail -1; git status --short",

<pasted_content id="4baa">
==> Caveats
==> libomp
libomp is keg-only, which means it was not symlinked into /opt/homebrew,
because it can override GCC headers and result in broken builds.

For compilers to find libomp you may need to set:
  export LDFLAGS=-L/opt/homebrew/opt/libomp/lib
  export CPPFLAGS=-I/opt/homebrew/opt/libomp/include
oleksandr@Oleksandrs-MacBook-Pro ~ %
